NDEL1

UniProt ID: Q9GZM8
Organism: Homo sapiens
Review Status: COMPLETE
📝 Provide Detailed Feedback
179 Annotations 1 Research 2 Core Functions 61 References 1 Documents 3 Questions 1 Experiments Raw YAML

Gene Description

NDEL1 (nudE neurodevelopment protein 1-like 1, formerly NUDEL) is a cytoplasmic coiled-coil protein of the NudE family and a paralog of NDE1. Its N-terminal coiled coil forms parallel homodimers (and tetramers) that bind the LIS1 (PAFAH1B1) homodimer, and its C-terminal region binds the N-terminus of the cytoplasmic dynein-1 intermediate chain and DISC1. By tethering LIS1 to dynein NDEL1 regulates the minus-end-directed motor, supporting dynein assembly with dynactin and cargo adaptors and dynein function under load, although in purified systems it can also restrain dynein activation. NDEL1 localizes to the centrosome (mother centriole), where it helps anchor microtubules and target pericentriolar proteins, to kinetochores via CENP-F, to the mitotic spindle, and to axons and growth cones. It is required for dynein-dependent processes including nucleus-centrosome coupling and nuclear translocation (nucleokinesis) in radially migrating cortical neurons, retrograde organelle and lysosome transport, Golgi integrity, and chromosome alignment and segregation. CDK5/p35 (and CDK1 in mitosis) phosphorylate its C-terminal region; phosphorylated NDEL1 recruits the microtubule-severing katanin p60 and switches LIS1/dynein regulation. Additional reported roles include neurofilament assembly, DISC1-dependent neurite outgrowth, adhesion at the leading edge of migrating cells, and enhancement of dynamin 2 GTPase activity.

Existing Annotations Review

GO Term Evidence Action Reason
GO:0000132 establishment of mitotic spindle orientation
IBA
GO_REF:0000033 		KEEP AS NON CORE
Summary: IBA (PAINT, NudE family node PTN000100519) transfer of establishment of mitotic spindle orientation, seeded by NDE1.
Reason: NudE-family proteins act with LIS1/dynein at the cell cortex and kinetochore during mitosis and NDEL1 localizes to the mitotic spindle, so inheritance of the NDE1-based node is phylogenetically reasonable. NDEL1's mitotic roles are partly redundant with NDE1 and not its principal function, so the annotation is kept as non-core.
Supporting Evidence:
PMID:12556484
A fraction of Nudel bound to centrosomes strongly in interphase and localized to mitotic spindles in early M phase
file:human/NDEL1/NDEL1-uniprot.txt
Required for mitosis in some cell types but appears to be dispensible for mitosis in cortical neuronal progenitors, which instead requires NDE1.
GO:0000776 kinetochore
IBA
GO_REF:0000033 		ACCEPT
Summary: IBA (PAINT) kinetochore localization for the NudE family; NDEL1 itself has direct IDA evidence (it appears in its own WITH/FROM, as expected).
Reason: Vergnolle and Taylor showed that CENP-F recruits NDEL1, NDE1 and LIS1 to kinetochores, linking the NudE/LIS1/dynein module to kinetochores in mitosis. Kinetochore is a well-supported location of NDEL1 and is recorded in UniProt.
Supporting Evidence:
PMID:17600710
We show that Ndel1, Nde1, and Lis1 localize to kinetochores in a Cenp-F-dependent manner

Summary: High-throughput or interactome-scale detection of an NDEL1 interaction with CCSER2 (Q9H7U1).
Reason: GO:0005515 protein binding carries no functional information. Per project policy generic protein binding is removed rather than kept; removal does not dispute the reported interaction. The informative activity of NDEL1 -- binding the dynein intermediate chain and recruiting LIS1 so as to regulate dynein -- is captured by the proposed NEW annotation to GO:0140659 cytoskeletal motor regulator activity and in core_functions. No more specific GO MF term describes binding to this partner. This row derives from a proteome-scale interaction screen and does not by itself indicate a biological activity of NDEL1.

GO:0005813 centrosome
IBA
GO_REF:0000033 		ACCEPT
Summary: NDEL1 is a centrosomal (mother centriole) protein.
Reason: Centrosome localization is supported by multiple independent studies in human and rodent cells: NDEL1 binds centrosomes strongly in interphase, is a mother-centriole protein required for centrosomal microtubule anchoring, and colocalizes with LIS1 at the centrosome of early neuroblasts. The IBA and the IEA mapping are both consistent.
Supporting Evidence:
PMID:12556484
A fraction of Nudel bound to centrosomes strongly in interphase and localized to mitotic spindles in early M phase
PMID:16291865
Silencing Nudel also represses centrosomal MT nucleation and anchoring.
PMID:11163259
LIS1 and NUDEL colocalize predominantly at the centrosome in early neuroblasts but redistribute to axons in association with retrograde dynein motor proteins

GO:0005871 kinesin complex
IBA
GO_REF:0000033 		REMOVE
Summary: IBA (PAINT) 'part of kinesin complex' seeded by rat Ndel1 (RGD:621235).
Reason: The underlying rat study (DISC1 regulates transport of the NUDEL/LIS1/14-3-3epsilon complex through kinesin-1) describes NDEL1 as cargo carried by kinesin-1 via the DISC1 adaptor, not as a structural subunit of a kinesin complex. NDEL1 has no kinesin-binding role in any other system and is a dynein regulator. Being transported by kinesin does not make a protein part of the kinesin complex, so the IBA overstates the relationship.
Propagation Review
Root cause: SOURCE BAD
Failure modes: ROLE CONFLATION
Sources checked:
PANTHER:PTN000100519 · PTN000100519 SOURCE BAD
Node assertion of kinesin complex membership rests only on rat Ndel1, whose experimental annotation records kinesin-1 cargo transport rather than subunit membership.
RGD:621235 SOURCE BAD
Rat Ndel1 IDA (DISC1/kinesin-1 transport study) conflates being carried by kinesin-1 with being part of the kinesin complex.
Supporting Evidence:
file:human/NDEL1/NDEL1-uniprot.txt
Also positively regulates the activity of the minus-end directed microtubule motor protein dynein.
GO:0005875 microtubule associated complex
IEA
GO_REF:0000107 		ACCEPT
Summary: Mapped microtubule associated complex from mouse Ndel1 (IDA, LIS1/NUDEL/dynein heavy chain complex).
Reason: NDEL1 forms a complex with LIS1 and cytoplasmic dynein heavy chain; microtubule associated complex is a correct, general description. NDEL1 is not a dynein subunit, so the general term is preferable to cytoplasmic dynein complex.

PMID:11163259
LIS1 directly interacts with the cytoplasmic dynein heavy chain (CDHC) and NUDEL
PMID:11163259
NUDEL and LIS1 regulate the distribution of CDHC along microtubules
GO:0007020 microtubule nucleation
IBA
GO_REF:0000033 		KEEP AS NON CORE
Summary: IBA (PAINT) microtubule nucleation, seeded by mouse Nde1.
Reason: Silencing NDEL1 reduces centrosomal gamma-tubulin and microtubule nucleation in HeLa cells, so the inherited annotation is supported. NDEL1 acts by targeting nucleating factors (pericentrin, gamma-tubulin) to the centrosome rather than nucleating microtubules itself, so this is kept as a non-core, indirect role.
Supporting Evidence:
PMID:16291865
Silencing Nudel also represses centrosomal MT nucleation and anchoring.
GO:0007059 chromosome segregation
IBA
GO_REF:0000033 		KEEP AS NON CORE
Summary: IBA (PAINT) chromosome segregation, with NDEL1's own IMP among the descendant evidence.
Reason: Ndel1-deficient HeLa cells show kinetochore malorientations and lagging chromosomes, supporting a role in chromosome segregation via the kinetochore LIS1/dynein pathway. NDEL1 is dispensable for mitosis in cortical progenitors, where NDE1 predominates, so the role is kept as non-core.
Supporting Evidence:
PMID:17600710
inhibition of Ndel1 results in malorientations that are not detected by the spindle checkpoint; Ndel1-deficient cells consequently enter anaphase in a timely manner but lagging chromosomes then manifest
file:human/NDEL1/NDEL1-uniprot.txt
Required for mitosis in some cell types but appears to be dispensible for mitosis in cortical neuronal progenitors, which instead requires NDE1.

GO:0008021 synaptic vesicle
IEA
GO_REF:0000107 		MARK AS OVER ANNOTATED
Summary: IEA transfer of synaptic vesicle localization from a single mouse IDA (Bradshaw et al. 2008, centrosome and synapse study).
Reason: NDEL1 is a cytosolic dynein regulator enriched at centrosomes, axons and growth cones; there is no human evidence that it is a synaptic vesicle component, and a single fractionation/immunostaining observation propagated by orthology does not represent a functional location.
Supporting Evidence:
PMID:11163259
LIS1 and NUDEL colocalize predominantly at the centrosome in early neuroblasts but redistribute to axons in association with retrograde dynein motor proteins
GO:0008286 insulin receptor signaling pathway
IEA
GO_REF:0000107 		MARK AS OVER ANNOTATED
Summary: IEA transfer of insulin receptor signaling pathway from a mouse IDA on insulin regulation of APC-dynein interaction.
Reason: NDEL1 is not a component of insulin receptor signaling; at most it is a downstream dynein-associated effector whose interactions are modulated by insulin signaling. Participation in a signaling pathway requires acting in signal transduction, which NDEL1 does not do.
Supporting Evidence:
file:human/NDEL1/NDEL1-uniprot.txt
Also positively regulates the activity of the minus-end directed microtubule motor protein dynein.

GO:0021799 cerebral cortex radially oriented cell migration
IEA
GO_REF:0000107 		ACCEPT
Summary: IEA transfer from mouse Ndel1 of cerebral cortex radially oriented cell migration.

PMID:38194050

GO:0042802 identical protein binding
IEA
GO_REF:0000107 		MODIFY
Summary: NDEL1 self-association (identical protein binding).
Reason: NDEL1 self-association is structurally defined: the N-terminal coiled coil forms a stable parallel homodimer (dimers further pair into tetramers). The more informative term protein homodimerization activity describes this self-interaction; it is structural rather than a core activity.
Proposed replacements: protein homodimerization activity
Supporting Evidence:
PMID:17997972
our structures reveal how the Ndel1 coiled coil forms a stable parallel homodimer

Core Functions

Binds the cytoplasmic dynein-1 intermediate chain and recruits LIS1 to dynein, regulating dynein assembly with dynactin/adaptors and its force production; drives dynein-dependent nuclear translocation in migrating neurons and minus-end organelle transport.

Suggested Questions for Experts

Q: Is NDEL1 net-activating or net-inhibitory for dynein in vivo, and does CDK5/CDK1 phosphorylation of its C-terminus switch between the two modes observed in reconstitution (LIS1 recruitment versus sequestration)?

Q: How are NDEL1 and NDE1 partitioned between postmitotic neuronal nucleokinesis and progenitor interkinetic nuclear migration, and does NDEL1 join the NUP133-CENPF nuclear-pore route in human radial glia?

Q: Is the reported serine oligopeptidase activity of NDEL1 (inhibited by DISC1) a genuine physiological enzymatic function with defined endogenous substrates, and is it separable from dynein regulation?

Suggested Experiments

Experiment: Live imaging of nuclear translocation in human iPSC-derived cortical neurons or organoids with NDEL1 knockout, rescued by LIS1-binding or dynein-IC-binding deficient NDEL1 mutants, to establish which interaction is required for nucleokinesis in human cells.

Human NDEL1 (Q9GZM8): Functional-Annotation Research Report Falcon Edison Scientific Literature 45 citations 1 artifacts 2026-09-27T16:22:05.145498
Edison artifact artifact-00 (text/markdown)

The research report should be a detailed narrative explaining the function, biological processes, and localization of the gene product. Citations should be given for all claims.

You should prioritize authoritative reviews and primary scientific literature when conducting research. You can supplement
this with annotations you find in gene/protein databases, but these can be outdated or inaccurate.

We are specifically interested in the primary function of the gene - for enzymes, what reaction is catalyzed, and what is the substrate specificity? For transporters, what is the substrate? For structural proteins or adapters, what is the broader structural role? For signaling molecules, what is the role in the pathway.

We are interested in where in or outside the cell the gene product carries out its function.

We are also interested in the signaling or biochemical pathways in which the gene functions. We are less interested in broad pleiotropic effects, except where these elucidate the precise role.

Include evidence where possible. We are interested in both experimental evidence as well as inference from structure, evolution, or bioinformatic analysis. Precise studies should be prioritized over high-throughput, where available.

Human NDEL1 (Q9GZM8): Functional-Annotation Research Report

Executive conclusion

Human NDEL1 encodes nuclear distribution protein nudE-like 1—also called NUDEL, protein Nudel, EOPA, or MITAP1. The specified identity is correct: NDEL1, UniProt Q9GZM8, Homo sapiens, a NudE-family protein containing the NUDE/NUDE_C architecture. It is distinct from the related paralog NDE1; the two human proteins are about 56% identical and 69% similar and have overlapping but nonidentical expression, interactions, and functions. Findings about NDE1 therefore should not automatically be assigned to NDEL1. Open Targets independently identifies human NDEL1 as ENSG00000166579, approved name “nudE neurodevelopment protein 1 like 1.” (OpenTargets Search: -NDEL1, garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 2-4)

The best-supported primary function of NDEL1 is not catalysis or transport of a substrate. It is a nonmotor, intracellular scaffold and spatial regulator of cytoplasmic dynein-1 and LIS1. NDEL1 helps recruit or position LIS1 and dynein, regulates assembly/activation of dynein–dynactin–adaptor complexes, and couples dynein force production to centrosomes, kinetochores, spindle poles, neuronal nuclei, axons, and selected cargo. A reported serine-oligopeptidase activity remains a less-established secondary function because physiologically validated endogenous substrates and its relationship to the dynein pathway remain uncertain. (garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 6-7, okada2023conservedrolesfor pages 6-7)

Topic Precise finding Evidence/model Interpretation/strength Source with year and DOI URL
Identity versus NDE1 NDEL1/NUDEL is human UniProt Q9GZM8, a NudE-family dynein regulator distinct from paralog NDE1. The paralogs are approximately 56% identical and 69% similar but differ in expression and some cellular roles. Sequence comparison, expression studies, genetics, interaction mapping, and paralog-specific depletion. Identity verified; strong evidence. Results for NDE1 must not automatically be assigned to NDEL1. Garrott et al., 2022, doi:10.3389/fcell.2022.871935 (garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 6-7, garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 2-4)
Architecture and dynein binding NDEL1 has an N-terminal coiled-coil/NUDE region and a comparatively disordered regulatory C terminus. Its N-terminal approximately 200 residues bind the dynein intermediate-chain N terminus; NDEL1 E48A disrupts this interaction, whose affinity is in the low-micromolar range. Mutagenesis, truncation mapping, binding assays, and structural modeling. Strong biochemical evidence, although multiple NDEL1 surfaces may contact the dynein complex. Garrott et al., 2022, doi:10.3389/fcell.2022.871935 (garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 6-7)
LIS1-binding interface The NDEL1 coiled-coil segment spanning approximately residues 85–169 binds LIS1; E116A or R127A abolishes binding. The interaction has high-nanomolar affinity. Targeted mutagenesis and quantitative protein-binding studies. Strong direct evidence for a defined NDEL1–LIS1 interface. Garrott et al., 2022, doi:10.3389/fcell.2022.871935 (garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 6-7)
Arg105 structural and disease relevance Arg105 lies within the coiled-coil region involved in self-association and LIS1 binding. The patient variant p.Arg105Pro disrupts LIS1 association and fails to support normal neuronal migration. Patient sequencing, co-immunoprecipitation, cell imaging, and in-utero electroporation. Strong variant-to-mechanism evidence, but based on only two unrelated patients. Tsai et al., 2024, doi:10.1007/s00401-023-02665-y (tsai2024novellissencephalyassociatedndel1 pages 1-2, tsai2024novellissencephalyassociatedndel1 pages 4-5)
Current dynein–LIS1 handoff model NDEL1 recruits LIS1 to dynein through the intermediate-chain N terminus, but NDEL1-bound LIS1 and motor-domain-bound LIS1 are not a stable simultaneous complex. NDEL1 and the dynein motor compete for an overlapping LIS1 surface, implying temporally separated recruitment and handoff before dynein–dynactin–adaptor activation. Purified human and yeast proteins, competition assays, mutagenesis, AlphaFold-based structural models, and evolutionary comparison. Leading peer-reviewed 2023 model. It revises simple stable-tripartite-scaffold models; cellular timing remains incompletely resolved. Okada et al., 2023, doi:10.1038/s41467-023-41466-5 (okada2023conservedrolesfor pages 6-7)
Older in-vitro motility model LIS1 suppressed dynein-driven microtubule gliding; NDEL1’s C terminus released dynein from microtubules, whereas its N terminus reversed LIS1-mediated suppression through LIS1 binding. Recombinant fragments and microtubule-gliding assays. Direct but assay-dependent evidence. These observations remain informative but should be interpreted alongside newer dynein-activation and handoff models. Torisawa et al., 2011, doi:10.1074/jbc.M110.169847 (torisawa2011functionaldissectionof pages 1-1)
Alternative inhibition model Purified NDEL1 inhibited dynein–dynactin–adaptor assembly and could sequester LIS1; C-terminal phosphomimetic substitutions increased inhibition. The authors proposed localized scaffolding of inactive dynein/LIS1 followed by regulated NDEL1 release. Quantitative binding, protein biochemistry, and single-molecule imaging. Preprint finding—preliminary. It highlights context dependence and has not superseded peer-reviewed cellular evidence that NDEL1 promotes dynein function. Garrott et al., 2023 bioRxiv, doi:10.1101/2023.01.25.525437 (garrott2023ndel1modulatesdynein pages 11-12)
Centrosomes and neuronal nucleokinesis NDEL1, LIS1, and dynein concentrate at centrosomes and microtubules in migrating neurons. Ndel1 depletion uncouples the centrosome from the nucleus, impairs nuclear translocation, and disrupts cortical neuronal positioning; LIS1 overexpression partially rescues Ndel1 depletion. Developing-neocortex RNA interference, localization, centrosome–nucleus measurements, and genetic rescue. Strong pathway evidence supporting a structural/regulatory role in dynein-generated nucleokinetic force. Shu et al., 2004, doi:10.1016/j.neuron.2004.09.030 (shu2004ndel1operatesin pages 1-2)
Kinetochores and spindle poles NDEL1/NDE1–LIS1–dynein complexes are recruited to kinetochores through CENP-F and contribute to spindle organization and checkpoint-protein transport toward spindle poles. Immunolocalization, depletion, interaction mapping, and mitotic phenotyping. Strong cellular evidence, although NDE1 can contribute more strongly than NDEL1 to kinetochore dynein recruitment in some systems. Vergnolle & Taylor, 2007, doi:10.1016/j.cub.2007.05.077; Garrott et al., 2022, doi:10.3389/fcell.2022.871935 (garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 6-7)
Ser285 and spatial mitotic control Pole-localized Aurora A phosphorylates NDEL1 at Ser285. Replacement with nonphosphorylatable S285A increases Mad1 and NuMA at spindle poles and weakens nocodazole-induced checkpoint arrest, suggesting that phosphorylation locally limits dynein transport or cargo retention near poles. HeLa T-REx replacement experiments, Aurora inhibitors, recombinant kinase assays, live imaging, and Xenopus aster assays. Strong mechanistic evidence for a phosphorylation-controlled spatial switch; whether phosphorylation releases cargo, NDEL1, or dynein from microtubules remains unresolved. Janczyk et al., 2023, doi:10.1091/mbc.e21-09-0438 (janczyk2023auroraaphosphorylates pages 1-2, janczyk2023auroraaphosphorylates pages 5-7, janczyk2023auroraaphosphorylates pages 4-5)
Axon initial segment and axonal cargo NDEL1 localizes at the axon initial segment, where LIS1-dependent dynein regulation supports polarized cargo sorting. In mature axons, disrupting NDEL1/LIS1 impairs retrograde mitochondrial transport and alters mitochondrial length, with evidence of cargo-specific requirements. Cultured neurons, knockdown and mutant rescue, live cargo imaging, and adult peripheral-nerve models. Moderate-to-strong experimental evidence; most work uses rodent neurons rather than human neurons in vivo. Kuijpers et al., 2016, doi:10.1016/j.neuron.2016.01.022; Pandey et al., 2022, doi:10.3389/fnmol.2022.841047 (garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 14-15)
2024 mosaic cortical-malformation cases Two unrelated patients with pachygyria, with or without subcortical band heterotopia, carried the same de novo postzygotic c.314G>C, p.Arg105Pro variant. Variant fractions were approximately 25–30% and 15–20%, respectively; the CADD score was 32. Exome/Sanger sequencing, patient phenotyping, transcriptomics, protein interaction assays, and neuronal-migration models. Important first human genotype–mechanism report, but recurrence in only two mosaic cases limits penetrance and prevalence estimates. Tsai et al., 2024, doi:10.1007/s00401-023-02665-y (tsai2024novellissencephalyassociatedndel1 pages 1-2, tsai2024novellissencephalyassociatedndel1 pages 4-5)
2024 autism cortical organoids ASD organoids were reported as 39–41% larger and grew nearly threefold faster than controls. NDEL1 activity correlated inversely with growth rate (Spearman ρ = −0.757, p = 0.014) and size (r = −0.697, p = 0.025); expression correlated inversely with size (r = −0.932, p = 0.006). A total of 4,910 organoids derived from 10 toddlers with ASD and 6 controls, with an independent production batch and linked clinical measures. Preliminary biomarker/model evidence. The subject-level sample is small, associations do not prove causation, and clinical diagnostic validity has not been established. Courchesne et al., 2024, doi:10.1186/s13229-024-00602-8 (courchesne2024embryonicoriginofa pages 12-13, courchesne2024embryonicoriginof pages 11-12)
2024 schizophrenia sodium-nitroprusside study Adjunctive sodium nitroprusside reduced measured NDEL1 oligopeptidase activity relative to placebo (t = 7.756, df = 97, p < 0.0001; Cohen’s d = 1.44) in a crossover study involving 15 patients, alongside results in spontaneously hypertensive rats. Double-blind randomized crossover infusions: 0.5 μg/kg/min for 4 hours, weekly for 4 weeks; biochemical activity assay and psychopathology scales. Preliminary biomarker/pharmacodynamic evidence. The clinical cohort was very small, and neither diagnostic performance nor evidence that NDEL1 is the drug’s causal target was established. Nani et al., 2024, doi:10.47626/1516-4446-2023-3315
Proposed oligopeptidase activity NDEL1 has been reported to cleave unstructured peptides as a serine oligopeptidase, with DISC1 inhibiting this activity; its physiological substrates and relationship to dynein regulation remain uncertain. In-vitro activity assays and disease-associated biomarker studies. Unresolved secondary function. NDEL1’s best-established primary role is as a nonmotor dynein/LIS1 scaffold-regulator, not as a validated metabolic enzyme with a defined endogenous substrate. Garrott et al., 2022, doi:10.3389/fcell.2022.871935 (garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 6-7)

Table: Compact evidence map distinguishing established NDEL1 dynein-regulatory mechanisms from evolving models and preliminary clinical biomarker findings. It integrates mapped residues, cellular locations, recent mechanistic research, and quantitative 2024 human data.

1. Identity, family, and structural organization

Mandatory identity verification

The literature consistently uses NDEL1, Ndel1, or the older name NUDEL for the protein matching human Q9GZM8. It belongs to the conserved NudE family and is a paralog—not a synonym—of NDE1. NDEL1 expression is comparatively sustained through life and is prominent in the nervous system, whereas NDE1 is more strongly associated with early proliferative stages. NDEL1 knockout is embryonically lethal in mice, while NDE1-null mice can survive, supporting overlapping but biologically nonredundant functions. (wynshawboris2001lis1anddynein pages 7-8, garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 2-4)

This matches the supplied InterPro/Pfam annotations—NUDE (IPR033494), NUDE_dom (IPR006964), and NUDE_C (PF04880)—and the experimentally established elongated coiled-coil architecture. No evidence was found that the requested symbol referred to a different organism or protein.

Domain-level functional map

NDEL1 contains an N-terminal NUDE/coiled-coil scaffold followed by a more flexible, phosphorylation-rich C-terminal region. The protein can form homodimers and heterodimers with NDE1, although their relative abundance in vivo is uncertain. The following experimentally mapped sites are especially informative:

  • Approximately residues 85–169 form a principal LIS1-binding region. NDEL1 substitutions E116A and R127A abolish LIS1 association; the interaction has high-nanomolar affinity.
  • The N-terminal approximately 200 residues bind the N-terminal segment of dynein intermediate chain, with E48A disrupting this interaction; measured affinity is in the low-micromolar range.
  • The flexible C-terminal region makes additional dynein contacts and contains regulatory phosphorylation sites.
  • Arg105, altered in the 2024 cortical-malformation cases, lies in the coiled-coil region involved in self-association and LIS1 binding.
  • Ser285 is an Aurora-regulated residue controlling mitotic dynein behavior near spindle poles. (garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 6-7, tsai2024novellissencephalyassociatedndel1 pages 4-5, janczyk2023auroraaphosphorylates pages 1-2)

2. Primary molecular function: regulation of dynein and LIS1

Cytoplasmic dynein-1 is the principal microtubule-minus-end motor in animal cells. Processive movement generally requires dynein, dynactin, and an activating cargo adaptor. LIS1 facilitates assembly of this activated complex. NDEL1 acts upstream and spatially within this pathway rather than functioning as the motor itself.

Current handoff model

The strongest recent mechanistic update is the September 2023 Nature Communications study by Okada and colleagues (DOI 10.1038/s41467-023-41466-5). Purified-protein, mutational, evolutionary, and structural-modeling results support the following sequence:

  1. NDEL1 binds the N terminus of dynein intermediate chain.
  2. It recruits or concentrates LIS1 near dynein.
  3. NDEL1 and the dynein motor domain contact overlapping surfaces on LIS1.
  4. LIS1 therefore must be transferred—or “handed off”—from NDEL1 to the motor in temporally distinct steps.
  5. Dynactin and an activating adaptor then stabilize the active transport complex.

This replaces an overly simple model in which NDEL1, LIS1, and the dynein motor remain together as one stable tripartite complex. Wild-type LIS1/Pac1 formed 1:1 dimeric complexes with NDEL1/Ndl1 in the biochemical system, and mutations in the LIS1/Pac1 motor-binding surface disrupted the competition relationship. (okada2023conservedrolesfor pages 6-7)

An independent 2023 preprint reported that purified NDEL1 can inhibit dynein–dynactin–adaptor assembly and sequester LIS1, with C-terminal phosphomimetic substitutions strengthening inhibition. Its proposed reconciliation is that NDEL1 locally scaffolds dynein and LIS1 in a primed but inactive state and must then be released for synchronized activation. Because this study was a preprint and purified systems omit many cellular regulators, this inhibitory model should be treated as provisional rather than as evidence that NDEL1 is globally a negative regulator in cells. (garrott2023ndel1modulatesdynein pages 11-12)

Relation to earlier motility results

Earlier microtubule-gliding experiments found that LIS1 suppressed dynein-mediated microtubule translocation, NDEL1’s C terminus promoted dynein dissociation from microtubules, and its N-terminal LIS1-binding region restored movement after LIS1 inhibition. These direct observations established region-specific effects but predated current understanding of activated dynein–dynactin–adaptor assembly. They are best interpreted as biochemical states sampled by NDEL1 during recruitment, inhibition, handoff, and release—not as one universal effect on motility. (torisawa2011functionaldissectionof pages 1-1)

Thus, authoritative expert reviews describe NDEL1’s precise molecular effect as context-dependent and still incompletely resolved. Its established role is to control where and when LIS1 and dynein interact; whether a particular assay observes activation, inhibition, microtubule release, or cargo recruitment depends on complex composition and phosphorylation state. (garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 6-7, garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 2-4)

3. Cellular localization and site-specific functions

NDEL1 is an intracellular, cytoplasmic/cytoskeletal protein rather than a secreted or membrane transporter. Its localization is dynamic and interaction-dependent.

Centrosomes and microtubule-organizing centers

NDEL1 colocalizes with LIS1 and dynein at centrosomes/MTOCs. In developing neurons it links microtubule-based dynein force to nucleus–centrosome coupling. Depletion of Ndel1, LIS1, or dynein in developing neocortex disrupts coupling between the centrosome and nucleus, impairs nuclear translocation, and causes abnormal neuronal positioning. LIS1 overexpression partially rescued Ndel1 depletion, whereas the reverse rescue did not occur, placing NDEL1 upstream of or alongside LIS1-mediated dynein regulation. (wynshawboris2001lis1anddynein pages 7-8, shu2004ndel1operatesin pages 1-2)

This mechanical process—nucleokinesis—is more precise than the broad annotation “neuronal migration.” The centrosome advances into the leading process; dynein-associated forces then move the nucleus toward it. NDEL1 organizes the LIS1–dynein machinery needed to maintain this mechanical coupling.

Kinetochores, spindle poles, and the mitotic checkpoint

NDEL1/NDE1–LIS1–dynein complexes are recruited to kinetochores through proteins including CENP-F. Dynein removes spindle-assembly-checkpoint components from attached kinetochores and transports them toward spindle poles, contributing to checkpoint silencing and spindle organization. NDE1 may make a larger contribution to kinetochore dynein recruitment than NDEL1 in some cultured-cell systems, emphasizing that the paralogs are not interchangeable. (garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 6-7)

A January 2023 Molecular Biology of the Cell study (DOI 10.1091/mbc.e21-09-0438) established a spatial phosphorylation switch. Aurora A at spindle poles phosphorylates NDEL1 Ser285. Aurora-A inhibition with 200 nM MLN8054 increased pole-associated Mad1, while replacement of endogenous NDEL1 with nonphosphorylatable S285A increased both Mad1 and NuMA accumulation at poles and weakened arrest in 20 nM nocodazole. The data support local down-regulation of NDEL1/dynein as complexes approach Aurora-A-rich poles, although whether phosphorylation releases cargo, releases NDEL1, or lowers dynein–microtubule engagement is unresolved. Experiments used human HeLa T-REx cells, Xenopus cells, recombinant kinase assays, and Xenopus egg-extract asters. (janczyk2023auroraaphosphorylates pages 1-2, janczyk2023auroraaphosphorylates pages 5-7, janczyk2023auroraaphosphorylates pages 4-5)

Neuronal axons and cargo selection

As neurons mature, NDEL1 and LIS1 occur in neuronal soma, growth-associated regions, and axons. NDEL1 is enriched at the axon initial segment, where it works with LIS1 and dynein to maintain polarized cargo sorting. In mature rodent sensory axons, NDEL1/LIS1 perturbation reduces retrograde mitochondrial trafficking and changes mitochondrial length. Acidic organelles and mitochondria show different NDEL1 requirements, indicating cargo-specific regulation rather than a universal requirement for every dynein cargo. (wynshawboris2001lis1anddynein pages 7-8, garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 14-15)

NDEL1 also contributes to Golgi and lipid-droplet positioning. Individual depletion of NDEL1 or NDE1 causes relatively mild Golgi dispersal, whereas combined depletion causes a stronger phenotype, demonstrating partial redundancy in non-neuronal dynein functions. (garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 6-7)

4. Biological pathways

The most defensible pathway-level annotation is:

NDEL1 → recruitment/positioning of LIS1 and dynein → LIS1 handoff to the dynein motor → dynein–dynactin–activating-adaptor assembly → minus-end-directed force or transport.

This module is deployed in several defined settings:

  1. Neuronal migration and nucleokinesis: force transmission between centrosome, microtubules, and nucleus.
  2. Mitotic spindle organization: pole focusing, centrosome behavior, and chromosome-associated dynein function.
  3. Spindle-assembly-checkpoint transport: removal of Mad1-containing checkpoint complexes from kinetochores, with Aurora-A/Ser285 regulation near poles.
  4. Axonal transport and polarity: retrograde transport and selective entry or exclusion of cargo at the axon initial segment.
  5. Organelle positioning: Golgi, lipid droplets, mitochondria, and selected endomembrane cargo.

NDEL1 is also regulated by kinases including Cdk5 and Aurora A. These phosphorylation events provide plausible switches coupling neural differentiation or cell-cycle state to dynein activity. (wynshawboris2001lis1anddynein pages 7-8, janczyk2023auroraaphosphorylates pages 1-2)

5. Enzymatic activity: evidence and caveats

NDEL1 has been reported to possess serine-oligopeptidase activity against unstructured peptides, and DISC1 can inhibit that activity. However, the retrieved authoritative review emphasizes that its physiological relevance to dynein regulation remains unresolved. A defined, validated endogenous substrate spectrum and an in-vivo catalytic mechanism comparable to established metabolic peptidases have not been demonstrated. (garrott2022nde1andndel1 pages 6-7)

Accordingly:

  • Primary annotation: nonenzymatic dynein/LIS1 scaffold-regulator.
  • Secondary/provisional annotation: serine oligopeptidase acting on unstructured peptides.
  • Substrate specificity: insufficiently defined for a confident physiological annotation.

Clinical studies measuring “NDEL1 activity” should therefore be understood as biomarker-assay studies, not proof that peptide cleavage is the protein’s principal cellular function.

6. Human disease evidence and 2023–2024 developments

First compelling NDEL1 cortical-malformation variant

The most important 2024 advance was the January Acta Neuropathologica report by Tsai and colleagues (DOI 10.1007/s00401-023-02665-y). Two unrelated patients with pachygyria, one with associated subcortical band heterotopia, carried the same de novo postzygotic NDEL1 c.314G>C, p.Arg105Pro variant. Mosaic fractions were approximately 25–30% and 15–20%, and the variant had a CADD score of 32. Both patients had developmental impairment and drug-resistant epilepsy. (tsai2024novellissencephalyassociatedndel1 pages 1-2, tsai2024novellissencephalyassociatedndel1 pages 4-5)

Mechanistically, p.Arg105Pro disrupted NDEL1–LIS1 binding. Ndel1 knockdown in the developing cortex impaired migration and was not rescued by the mutant; mutant expression itself lengthened the neuronal leading process and disrupted nucleus–centrosome coupling. Single-cell and spatial analyses showed NDE1 enriched in cycling ventricular-zone radial glia, while NDEL1 was more prominent in postmitotic neurons of the intermediate zone and cortical plate. This explains why NDE1 loss is strongly associated with progenitor/microcephaly phenotypes, whereas NDEL1 dysfunction can preferentially impair postmitotic migration and cortical lamination. (tsai2024novellissencephalyassociatedndel1 pages 1-2)

The result is mechanistically strong but clinically early: only two mosaic cases were reported, so prevalence, penetrance, and the range of germline versus mosaic NDEL1 phenotypes remain unknown.

Autism cortical-organoid study

A May 2024 Molecular Autism study (DOI 10.1186/s13229-024-00602-8) analyzed 4,910 cortical organoids derived from 10 toddlers with autism and 6 controls. ASD organoids were 39–41% larger in two production batches and grew nearly three times faster. NDEL1 activity correlated inversely with organoid growth rate (Spearman ρ = −0.757, p = 0.014) and size (Pearson r = −0.697, p = 0.025); expression correlated inversely with size (r = −0.932, p = 0.006). Larger organoids were associated with more severe social phenotypes. (courchesne2024embryonicoriginofa pages 12-13, courchesne2024embryonicoriginof pages 11-12)

These are provocative patient-derived model data, not evidence that NDEL1 deficiency causes autism generally. The subject-level sample was small, multiple developmental pathways can affect organoid size, and neither diagnostic sensitivity nor specificity was established.

Schizophrenia pharmacodynamic study

A 2024 Brazilian Journal of Psychiatry study (DOI 10.47626/1516-4446-2023-3315) measured NDEL1 oligopeptidase activity after sodium-nitroprusside treatment. Fifteen patients participated in a double-blind randomized crossover experiment using 0.5 μg/kg/min for four hours weekly over four weeks. Activity was lower after sodium nitroprusside than placebo (t = 7.756, df = 97, p < 0.0001; Cohen’s d = 1.44), with parallel rodent observations.

This is preliminary pharmacodynamic-biomarker evidence. The cohort was extremely small; the result does not establish diagnostic validity, clinical utility, or NDEL1 as the causal drug target.

7. Current applications and translational status

There is presently no established NDEL1-directed therapy or routine clinical diagnostic test. Current applications are primarily research-oriented:

  • Genetic interpretation: NDEL1 should be considered in mosaic sequencing of unexplained pachygyria, subcortical band heterotopia, epilepsy, and migration-pattern cortical malformations, especially when standard germline testing is negative. The p.Arg105Pro report supports deep sequencing across affected and accessible tissues.
  • Mechanistic disease modeling: patient-derived organoids and in-utero electroporation allow direct testing of NDEL1-dependent proliferation, neuronal migration, and nucleus–centrosome mechanics.
  • Dynein-regulation assays: mapped NDEL1 interfaces and phosphosites provide tools for dissecting LIS1 recruitment, handoff, and activation.
  • Exploratory biomarkers: measured oligopeptidase activity has been studied in schizophrenia and autism models, but is not clinically validated.
  • Potential therapeutic logic: rather than inhibiting NDEL1 globally—which would risk disrupting mitosis, neuronal transport, and organelle positioning—future strategies would likely need to target a specific interface, phosphorylation state, tissue, or disease-associated variant.

Open Targets lists NDEL1 associations with several phenotypic terms, but the displayed evidence consists primarily of ClinVar-derived records and moderate association scores rather than validated drug-target relationships. These annotations should not be interpreted as proof of therapeutic tractability. (OpenTargets Search: -NDEL1)

8. Evidence-weighted functional annotation

Recommended concise annotation:

NDEL1 is a conserved intracellular coiled-coil scaffold and regulator of cytoplasmic dynein-1. It binds the dynein intermediate chain and LIS1, recruits and spatially organizes these proteins, and facilitates a regulated LIS1-to-dynein handoff preceding dynein–dynactin–adaptor activation. Through this mechanism, NDEL1 controls centrosome–nucleus coupling and neuronal nucleokinesis, mitotic spindle and checkpoint-protein transport, axonal polarity and retrograde cargo movement, and selected organelle-positioning events. Its activity is modulated by phosphorylation, including Aurora-A-dependent Ser285 phosphorylation at spindle poles. A reported serine-oligopeptidase activity has uncertain physiological substrates and should be treated as secondary/provisional.

9. Principal uncertainties

  1. The exact cellular sequence and kinetics of NDEL1 recruitment, LIS1 handoff, NDEL1 release, and dynein activation remain incompletely resolved.
  2. NDEL1 can appear activating or inhibitory in different purified systems; these outcomes likely reflect different biochemical states rather than a single constitutive effect.
  3. Functional redundancy with NDE1 varies by tissue, developmental stage, and cargo.
  4. The endogenous substrates and biological importance of reported oligopeptidase activity remain uncertain.
  5. Human monogenic evidence is newly compelling but still based on two mosaic p.Arg105Pro cases.
  6. Psychiatric and organoid biomarker findings are hypothesis-generating and not ready for clinical use.

📚 Additional Documentation

Notes

(NDEL1-notes.md)

NDEL1 review notes

Journal for the NDEL1 (Q9GZM8, NudE-like 1, formerly NUDEL) GO annotation review.

Sources used

  • UniProt Q9GZM8 (NDEL1-uniprot.txt).
  • Cached publications (GOA-cited plus additional papers cached for this review after PubMed eutils
    verification: PMID:12556484, PMID:14970193, PMID:16291865, PMID:16203747, PMID:20403325,
    PMID:37730751, PMID:37086789).
  • Deep research (NDEL1-deep-research-falcon.md) was not present when the review was first drafted;
    see the end of this file for the final check.

Biology summary (with provenance)

  • Coiled-coil NudE-family protein; the N-terminal coiled coil forms a parallel homodimer that binds LIS1
    PMID:17997972.
    The C-terminal region binds dynein and DISC1 PMID:22843697.
  • Core mechanism: tethers LIS1 to cytoplasmic dynein and regulates dynein. Rat/mouse cortical RNAi:
    PMID:15473966.
    Single-molecule: PMID:20403325.
    Dynein IC N-terminus binds Ndel1, which recruits LIS1 PMID:37730751.
    With purified human proteins Ndel1 can also inhibit dynein-dynactin-adaptor assembly
    PMID:37086789. So "cytoskeletal
    motor regulator activity" (GO:0140659) captures the activity better than any activator/inhibitor term.
  • Neuronal migration / nucleokinesis (mouse): PMID:20007476; PMID:15473966.
  • CDK5/p35 substrate PMID:11163260; phospho-NDEL1 recruits katanin p60
    PMID:16203747; Cdk5
    phosphorylation switches Lis1/Ndel1/dynein axonal transport PMID:22114287.
  • Centrosome: mother-centriole protein, needed for centrosomal MT nucleation/anchoring (human HeLa/Cos7)
    PMID:16291865.
  • Mitosis: kinetochore via CENP-F; Ndel1-depleted cells have lagging chromosomes PMID:17600710; spindle
    localization and M-phase phosphorylation PMID:12556484. UniProt notes NDEL1 is dispensable for mitosis in
    cortical progenitors (NDE1 dominant), so mitotic roles treated as non-core.
  • Membrane traffic: dynein-driven lysosome minus-end motility and Golgi integrity PMID:14970193.
  • Peripheral: Dyn2 GTPase enhancement PMID:21283621; paxillin/nascent adhesions PMID:19492042;
    DISC1-NDEL1 neurite outgrowth PMID:17035248.

Decisions

  • 118 GO:0005515 protein binding rows: REMOVE as uninformative (per project policy). The informative
    functions (dynein regulation via LIS1 recruitment) are captured by NEW GO:0140659 and core_functions.
  • Kinesin complex (IBA): REMOVE -- NDEL1 is kinesin-1 cargo (via DISC1/14-3-3), not a kinesin subunit.
  • Insulin receptor signaling (IEA) and synaptic vesicle (IEA): MARK_AS_OVER_ANNOTATED.
  • Identical protein binding (all 6 rows): MODIFY to protein homodimerization activity (structurally defined coiled-coil homodimer).
  • NEW: GO:0140659 cytoskeletal motor regulator activity (human proteins in vitro, PMID:37086789);
    GO:0030473 nuclear migration along microtubule (comparator: LIS1/PAFAH1B1 carries GO:0007097; NDEL1
    contributes regulator activity to the dynein step, so passes the participation test);
    GO:0034454 microtubule anchoring at centrosome (human HeLa RNAi, PMID:16291865).

Deep research check (final)

  • NDEL1-deep-research-falcon.md appeared during the review and was read. It agrees with the dynein/LIS1
    scaffold-regulator model and the handoff model (PMID:37730751). Additions taken up after verification:
  • Tsai et al. 2024 (PMID:38194050, verified via PubMed, cached full text): de novo mosaic NDEL1 p.R105P in
    pachygyria/SBH disrupts LIS1 binding and nucleokinesis PMID:38194050; NDE1 predominates in progenitors, NDEL1 in postmitotic neurons.
    Used to support NEW GO:0030473 and the radial migration row.
  • Reported serine oligopeptidase activity (DISC1-inhibited) -- not annotated; raised as a suggested question.
  • Correction to the deep research: it calls Garrott et al. 2023 a preprint; the work is published
    (PMID:37086789, J Biol Chem 2023).

📄 View Raw YAML

 
id: Q9GZM8
gene_symbol: NDEL1
product_type: PROTEIN
status: COMPLETE
taxon:
  id: NCBITaxon:9606
  label: Homo sapiens
description: NDEL1 (nudE neurodevelopment protein 1-like 1, formerly NUDEL) is a cytoplasmic coiled-coil
  protein of the NudE family and a paralog of NDE1. Its N-terminal coiled coil forms parallel homodimers
  (and tetramers) that bind the LIS1 (PAFAH1B1) homodimer, and its C-terminal region binds the N-terminus
  of the cytoplasmic dynein-1 intermediate chain and DISC1. By tethering LIS1 to dynein NDEL1 regulates
  the minus-end-directed motor, supporting dynein assembly with dynactin and cargo adaptors and dynein
  function under load, although in purified systems it can also restrain dynein activation. NDEL1 localizes
  to the centrosome (mother centriole), where it helps anchor microtubules and target pericentriolar proteins,
  to kinetochores via CENP-F, to the mitotic spindle, and to axons and growth cones. It is required for
  dynein-dependent processes including nucleus-centrosome coupling and nuclear translocation (nucleokinesis)
  in radially migrating cortical neurons, retrograde organelle and lysosome transport, Golgi integrity,
  and chromosome alignment and segregation. CDK5/p35 (and CDK1 in mitosis) phosphorylate its C-terminal
  region; phosphorylated NDEL1 recruits the microtubule-severing katanin p60 and switches LIS1/dynein
  regulation. Additional reported roles include neurofilament assembly, DISC1-dependent neurite outgrowth,
  adhesion at the leading edge of migrating cells, and enhancement of dynamin 2 GTPase activity.

- term:
    id: GO:0000132
    label: establishment of mitotic spindle orientation
  evidence_type: IBA
  original_reference_id: GO_REF:0000033
  qualifier: involved_in
  supporting_entities:
  - PANTHER:PTN000100519
  - UniProtKB:Q9NXR1
  review:
    summary: IBA (PAINT, NudE family node PTN000100519) transfer of establishment of mitotic spindle orientation,
      seeded by NDE1.
    action: KEEP_AS_NON_CORE
    reason: NudE-family proteins act with LIS1/dynein at the cell cortex and kinetochore during mitosis
      and NDEL1 localizes to the mitotic spindle, so inheritance of the NDE1-based node is phylogenetically
      reasonable. NDEL1's mitotic roles are partly redundant with NDE1 and not its principal function,
      so the annotation is kept as non-core.
    supported_by:
    - &id002
      reference_id: PMID:12556484
      supporting_text: A fraction of Nudel bound to centrosomes strongly in interphase and localized to
        mitotic spindles in early M phase
    - &id006
      reference_id: file:human/NDEL1/NDEL1-uniprot.txt
      supporting_text: Required for mitosis in some cell types but appears to be dispensible for mitosis
        in cortical neuronal progenitors, which instead requires NDE1.

    summary: IBA (PAINT) kinetochore localization for the NudE family; NDEL1 itself has direct IDA evidence
      (it appears in its own WITH/FROM, as expected).
    action: ACCEPT
    reason: Vergnolle and Taylor showed that CENP-F recruits NDEL1, NDE1 and LIS1 to kinetochores, linking
      the NudE/LIS1/dynein module to kinetochores in mitosis. Kinetochore is a well-supported location
      of NDEL1 and is recorded in UniProt.

- term:
    id: GO:0005871
    label: kinesin complex
  evidence_type: IBA
  original_reference_id: GO_REF:0000033
  qualifier: part_of
  supporting_entities:
  - PANTHER:PTN000100519
  - RGD:621235
  review:
    summary: IBA (PAINT) 'part of kinesin complex' seeded by rat Ndel1 (RGD:621235).
    action: REMOVE
    reason: The underlying rat study (DISC1 regulates transport of the NUDEL/LIS1/14-3-3epsilon complex
      through kinesin-1) describes NDEL1 as cargo carried by kinesin-1 via the DISC1 adaptor, not as a
      structural subunit of a kinesin complex. NDEL1 has no kinesin-binding role in any other system and
      is a dynein regulator. Being transported by kinesin does not make a protein part of the kinesin
      complex, so the IBA overstates the relationship.
    supported_by:
    - &id010
      reference_id: file:human/NDEL1/NDEL1-uniprot.txt
      supporting_text: Also positively regulates the activity of the minus-end directed microtubule motor
        protein dynein.
    propagation_review:
      root_cause: SOURCE_BAD
      failure_modes:
      - ROLE_CONFLATION
      source_entities:
      - source_id: PANTHER:PTN000100519
        source_label: PTN000100519
        source_status: SOURCE_BAD
        comment: Node assertion of kinesin complex membership rests only on rat Ndel1, whose experimental
          annotation records kinesin-1 cargo transport rather than subunit membership.
      - source_id: RGD:621235
        source_status: SOURCE_BAD
        comment: Rat Ndel1 IDA (DISC1/kinesin-1 transport study) conflates being carried by kinesin-1
          with being part of the kinesin complex.

- term:
    id: GO:0007020
    label: microtubule nucleation
  evidence_type: IBA
  original_reference_id: GO_REF:0000033
  qualifier: involved_in
  supporting_entities:
  - MGI:MGI:1914453
  - PANTHER:PTN000100519
  review:
    summary: IBA (PAINT) microtubule nucleation, seeded by mouse Nde1.
    action: KEEP_AS_NON_CORE
    reason: Silencing NDEL1 reduces centrosomal gamma-tubulin and microtubule nucleation in HeLa cells,

      a non-core, indirect role.

    action: KEEP_AS_NON_CORE
    reason: Ndel1-deficient HeLa cells show kinetochore malorientations and lagging chromosomes, supporting

        then manifest

    label: nuclear migration along microtubule
  evidence_type: ISS
  original_reference_id: PMID:38194050
  review:
    summary: NDEL1 is required, as a LIS1/dynein regulator, for dynein-driven nuclear translocation in

    action: NEW
    reason: 'Participation test: the dynein motor does the pulling, and NDEL1 contributes the regulatory

      of Ndel1 abolishes nuclear movement and uncouples nucleus and centrosome in mouse cortex; the evidence
      is from mouse and rat, transferred to the conserved human ortholog. A de novo human NDEL1 variant
      (p.R105P) found in pachygyria/subcortical band heterotopia abolishes LIS1 binding and, expressed
      in mouse cortex, impairs nucleus-centrosome coupling and nucleokinesis.'

- id: file:human/NDEL1/NDEL1-notes.md
  title: NDEL1 review notes
  findings: []
core_functions:
- description: Binds the cytoplasmic dynein-1 intermediate chain and recruits LIS1 to dynein, regulating
    dynein assembly with dynactin/adaptors and its force production; drives dynein-dependent nuclear translocation
    in migrating neurons and minus-end organelle transport.
  molecular_function:
    id: GO:0140659
    label: cytoskeletal motor regulator activity
  directly_involved_in:
  - id: GO:0030473
    label: nuclear migration along microtubule
  - id: GO:0021799
    label: cerebral cortex radially oriented cell migration
  - id: GO:0047496
    label: vesicle transport along microtubule
  locations:
  - id: GO:0005813
    label: centrosome
  - id: GO:0030424
    label: axon
  supported_by:
  - *id022
  - *id023
  - *id021
  - *id012
  - *id020
  - *id015
- description: At the mother centriole NDEL1 tethers dynein/dynactin and targets pericentriolar proteins,
    supporting microtubule anchoring at the centrosome.
  molecular_function:
    id: GO:0060090
    label: molecular adaptor activity
  directly_involved_in:
  - id: GO:0034454
    label: microtubule anchoring at centrosome
  locations:
  - id: GO:0005813
    label: centrosome
  supported_by:
  - *id003
  - *id024
suggested_questions:
- question: Is NDEL1 net-activating or net-inhibitory for dynein in vivo, and does CDK5/CDK1 phosphorylation
    of its C-terminus switch between the two modes observed in reconstitution (LIS1 recruitment versus
    sequestration)?
- question: How are NDEL1 and NDE1 partitioned between postmitotic neuronal nucleokinesis and progenitor
    interkinetic nuclear migration, and does NDEL1 join the NUP133-CENPF nuclear-pore route in human radial
    glia?
- question: Is the reported serine oligopeptidase activity of NDEL1 (inhibited by DISC1) a genuine physiological
    enzymatic function with defined endogenous substrates, and is it separable from dynein regulation?
suggested_experiments:
- description: Live imaging of nuclear translocation in human iPSC-derived cortical neurons or organoids
    with NDEL1 knockout, rescued by LIS1-binding or dynein-IC-binding deficient NDEL1 mutants, to establish
    which interaction is required for nucleokinesis in human cells.
